It may also be used consistent with the principles of fair use under the copyright law. Hereditary tubo-ovarian cancer risk assessment has evolved from a BRCA1/2 -centered consultation to an integrated prevention pathway that begins at diagnosis and extends to unaffected relatives. Germline testing is now justified for patients with epithelial tubo-ovarian cancer because actionable pathogenic variants are common enough that family-history-only referral misses many carriers. The central clinical challenge is no longer whether testing matters, but whether testing is interpreted and implemented in a way that prevents cancer. BRCA1 and BRCA2 remain the highest-impact tubo-ovarian cancer predisposition genes, but Lynch syndrome genes, BRIP1, RAD51C, RAD51D, PALB2 , and selected rare histology-directed syndromes require distinct counseling. Gene-specific penetrance, age-specific risk, family history, comorbidities, reproductive plans, and menopause consequences should shape the timing of risk-reducing surgery. Tumor testing improves therapeutic selection and can identify possible hereditary variants, but tumor-only testing does not replace germline testing when hereditary evaluation is indicated. Cascade testing should be measured and resourced as part of tubo-ovarian cancer care rather than treated as a passive family responsibility. Mainstream testing, traceback programs, and navigation-supported cascade testing offer pragmatic implementation models, but access gaps remain.
[w77ee86-2] For gynecologic oncology clinicians, modern hereditary risk assessment should be understood as a multidisciplinary prevention intervention: testing is the entry point, not the endpoint. This narrative review synthesizes gene-specific counseling, tumor–germline interpretation, and implementation strategies into a clinically oriented pathway from diagnosis to prevention for patients and relatives. ovarian cancer genetic testing hereditary cancer BRCA1 BRCA2 Lynch syndrome cascade testing pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access no pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript yes pmc-prop-open-manuscript yes pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement no pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes Introduction Hereditary tubo-ovarian cancer risk assessment has become a core competency in gynecologic oncology. The original clinical model - referral for BRCA1/2 counseling when the family history was striking - was appropriate for an earlier era of limited testing and narrower therapeutic relevance, but it is no longer sufficient. Multigene germline testing, tumor genomic testing, homologous recombination deficiency testing, and expanding risk-reducing surgical strategies have changed the clinical question from “Who qualifies for testing?” to “How does testing inform precise prevention for patients and relatives?” Current guidance from major oncology, pathology, and genetics organizations supports systematic testing after epithelial tubo-ovarian cancer and selected rare non-epithelial tumors with syndrome-specific associations, coordinated tumor-germline workflows, and gene-specific management of inherited risk. 1 - 5
[w77ee86-3] The hereditary tubo-ovarian cancer risk assessment is not merely a laboratory exercise. It is a longitudinal prevention pathway that includes test selection, interpretation, counseling, risk-reducing surgery when appropriate, and cascade testing. When any component fails - a patient is not offered testing, a tumor-only result is misread as excluding inherited risk, a variant of uncertain significance is overinterpreted, or relatives are not supported to test - the preventive value of the pathway is lost. 2 , 6
[w77ee86-4] Despite the evolving evidence base, a gap persists between guideline recommendations and clinical practice. Guidelines define who should be tested and how gene-specific risk should be managed, but translating that framework into a reliable prevention pathway — one that reaches patients, informs relatives, and adapts to implementation constraints — remains a practical challenge. This narrative review is intended as a clinical reference for physicians at the intersection of diagnosis, treatment, and prevention. Its objective is to integrate gene-specific counseling, tumor–germline interpretation, prevention timing, cascade testing, and implementation strategies rather than to replace local or national guidelines.
[w77ee86-5] Scope and approach This is a clinically oriented narrative review. We synthesize major international guidance, including major guideline recommendations, together with selected primary studies that clarify penetrance, test interpretation, prevention, and implementation. Guideline-based recommendations are distinguished from areas requiring individualized counseling, and jurisdiction-specific eligibility, consent, and management requirements remain applicable. We address the genes and syndromes most relevant to tubo-ovarian cancer diagnosis and prevention, the distinction between therapeutic and hereditary testing, interpretation of common result categories, gene-specific timing of risk-reducing surgery, limitations of risk models, and implementation strategies that can increase equitable access.
[w77ee86-6] Why hereditary risk assessment matters in tubo-ovarian cancer prevention Tubo-ovarian cancer remains a lethal malignancy worldwide; GLOBOCAN 2022 estimated approximately 324,000 new cases and 207,000 deaths globally, reflecting both late-stage presentation and limited effectiveness of early detection at population level. 7 Inherited predisposition accounts for a clinically important fraction of epithelial tubo-ovarian cancer. Contemporary series 8 and guidelines estimate that approximately 15-20% of epithelial tubo-ovarian cancers are associated with pathogenic or likely pathogenic germline variants in tubo-ovarian cancer susceptibility genes, most commonly BRCA1 and BRCA2 , but also RAD51C , RAD51D , BRIP1 , PALB2 , and the Lynch syndrome genes MLH1 , MSH2 , MSH6 , including EPCAM deletions that cause MSH2 silencing. 2 , 9 This prevalence is high enough to justify systematic germline testing rather than relying on family history alone.
[w77ee86-7] Family history is an imperfect screening tool for hereditary risk. Small family size, paternal transmission, adoption, incomplete knowledge of relatives, early deaths, hysterectomy or oophorectomy in relatives, and under-documentation of cancer diagnoses all reduce sensitivity. Family-history criteria may also amplify disparities when access to accurate family history or genetic services differs across ancestry, language, geography, and socioeconomic status. 3 , 9 Beyond these limitations, the case for systematic testing extends to its direct clinical value: for the affected patient, germline results may influence treatment selection, second-cancer risk management, and eligibility for trials. For relatives, the same result may create the opportunity to prevent tubo-ovarian, breast, endometrial, colorectal, pancreatic, or prostate cancer depending on the gene. 10 , 11
[w77ee86-8] Identifying carriers, however, is only valuable if it leads to action. Tubo-ovarian cancer screening is not an effective approach for prevention in high-risk carriers. Transvaginal ultrasonography and cancer antigen 125-based screening have not demonstrated a mortality benefit in the general population, including in the final UKCTOCS analysis, and have not established a survival benefit in high-risk carriers. Guidelines therefore regard such surveillance, if used, as an interim option for selected high-risk individuals who defer risk-reducing surgery rather than as a prevention strategy. 4 , 12 , 13 Therefore, the practical value of hereditary risk assessment is most evident when it enables risk-reducing surgery, breast and colorectal surveillance where indicated, chemoprevention or contraception discussions, reproductive counseling, and cascade testing.
[w77ee86-9] Who should be tested and referred All patients with epithelial ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer should be offered germline genetic testing that includes BRCA1 and BRCA2, and if available, other clinically relevant ovarian cancer susceptibility genes, including BRIP1 , RAD51C , RAD51D , PALB2 , MLH1 , MSH2 , MSH6 , and PMS2 . 1 - 3 , 5 American Society of Clinical Oncology (ASCO) recommends germline testing for all women diagnosed with epithelial ovarian cancer and somatic tumor testing for BRCA1/2 in those without a germline pathogenic variant; it also recommends mismatch repair deficiency testing for clear cell, endometrioid, or mucinous ovarian cancers. 2 The 2024 Society of Gynecologic Oncology clinical practice statement is concordant and emphasizes coordinated germline and somatic testing, while ACOG recommends routine hereditary cancer risk assessment and referral when increased risk is identified. 14 , 15 The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) similarly recommends pre-test counseling and germline testing for invasive epithelial ovarian cancer and for rare ovarian tumors with specific hereditary associations, including Sertoli-Leydig cell tumor, small cell carcinoma of the ovary hypercalcemic type, ovarian sex cord tumor with annular tubules, embryonal rhabdomyosarcoma of the ovary, and gynandroblastoma. 3 ESMO and the ESGO-ESMO-ESP consensus likewise support systematic germline testing and coordinated molecular assessment in epithelial tubo-ovarian cancer. 4 , 16 Borderline ovarian tumors are not, by themselves, a universal indication for germline testing; testing should instead be guided by personal and family history, tumor features, and jurisdiction-specific recommendations. 17
[w77ee86-10] Referral pathways should be broader than the test order itself and should define responsibility at each step. In a mainstream testing model, the treating gynecologic oncology team may initiate pre-test education, obtain consent according to local requirements, order germline testing, document the test indication and result in the oncology record, and ensure that results are communicated to the patient and relevant clinicians. However, mainstream testing should not replace genetics expertise. Genetics professionals should be involved for pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants, variants of uncertain significance that affect counseling, discordant tumor–germline results, negative results despite a strong or verified family history, rare tumors suggesting a syndrome-specific workup, complex reproductive or risk-reducing surgery decisions, and any situation in which the treating team cannot provide gene-specific counseling. Importantly, variants of uncertain significance should not be used to guide risk-reducing surgery or predictive testing in relatives unless reclassified. Finally, referral pathways should define how confirmed hereditary findings will be translated into cascade testing for at-risk relatives, while also specifying how unaffected individuals should be evaluated when no familial pathogenic variant is known but the cancer pattern in the family or risk-model estimates still suggest inherited risk. 1 , 3
[w77ee86-11] In practice, the most important quality metric is not simply whether testing is available. It is whether every eligible patient receives a clear recommendation, can access testing in a timely manner, receives an interpretable result, and has a route for relatives to act on it. Population-based testing in unaffected individuals without a known familial pathogenic variant is a separate question from universal testing after a cancer diagnosis. Such programs can identify carriers missed by family-history criteria, but eligibility, panel composition, consent, downstream capacity, and cost-effectiveness depend on the population and health system. 9
[w77ee86-12] Tubo-ovarian cancer predisposition genes and syndromes BRCA1 and BRCA2 BRCA1 and BRCA2 remain the highest-impact tubo-ovarian cancer predisposition genes, but counseling should present penetrance as an age-dependent estimate rather than a fixed individual risk. In a large prospective analysis of BRCA1/2 carriers, the cumulative tubo-ovarian cancer risk to age 80 years was estimated at 44% for BRCA1 carriers and 17% for BRCA2 carriers, while cumulative breast cancer risk to age 80 years was 72% and 69%, respectively . 18 These estimates have uncertainty around them, are influenced by the carrier populations studied, and do not imply uniform risk across the life course.
[w77ee86-13] Risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy is associated with substantial reduction in ovarian, fallopian tube, and primary peritoneal cancer risk and with reduced mortality in BRCA1/2 carriers. 19 , 20 Because BRCA1 -associated risk rises earlier, discussion of surgery generally begins after childbearing at age 35–40 years for BRCA1 pathogenic variant carriers and age 40–45 years for BRCA2 pathogenic variant carriers. The precise timing, however, should be individualized by integrating age, family history, prior cancer history, reproductive plans, and comorbidities rather than applying a fixed threshold. The consequences of premenopausal oophorectomy—including vasomotor symptoms, sexual dysfunction, bone loss, cardiovascular and cognitive concerns, infertility, and psychological effects—require explicit discussion before surgery. Hormone therapy after premenopausal risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy should be individualized in specialist counseling. Decisions should account for personal breast cancer history, breast tumor hormone receptor status when applicable, uterus status and the need for progestin with systemic estrogen, contraindications to hormone therapy, route and dose, severity of menopausal symptoms, bone and cardiovascular health, and patient preferences. In BRCA1/2 carriers without contraindications and without a personal history of estrogen receptor-positive breast cancer, hormone therapy until the average age of natural menopause may be considered after individualized risk–benefit counseling, and can start early after surgery. 4 , 12
[w77ee86-14] Lynch syndrome genes: MLH1, MSH2/EPCAM, MSH6, PMS2 Lynch syndrome counseling must be gene specific. Prospective Lynch Syndrome Database data demonstrated that gynecologic cancer risks differ materially across mismatch repair genes, with ovarian cancer risk generally higher for MLH1 and MSH2 , variable for MSH6 , and lower or uncertain for PMS2 . 21 , 22 In the 2020 gene-specific Prospective Lynch Syndrome Database analysis, cumulative ovarian cancer incidence to age 75 years was approximately 11% for MLH1 , 17% for MSH2 , 11% for MSH6 , and 3% for PMS2 , although confidence intervals were wide for some estimates. 21
[w77ee86-15] EPCAM deletions that involve the 3′ end can permit transcriptional read-through into MSH2 and cause allele-specific MSH2 promoter hypermethylation; cancer risk varies with deletion size and location, so interpretation requires genetics expertise. 23 Co-occurring pathogenic variants in BRCA1/2 and a mismatch repair gene are rare; available case-series data suggest that each syndrome should be interpreted and managed on its own evidence base. 24 Synchronous endometrial and ovarian cancers, particularly endometrioid tumors, are enriched for mismatch repair deficiency and Lynch syndrome and should prompt tumor mismatch repair assessment and germline evaluation when indicated. 25
[w77ee86-16] Risk-reducing gynecologic surgery for Lynch syndrome differs from BRCA -associated prevention because endometrial cancer prevention is a central objective and ovarian cancer risk is more gene-dependent. Hysterectomy after completion of childbearing is commonly discussed to reduce endometrial cancer risk, particularly for carriers of pathogenic variants of MLH1, MSH2, and EPCAM deletions, often from the mid-to-late thirties, with later or more individualized timing for MSH6 and PMS2, and adjustment for family history, colorectal cancer history, surgical history, and patient preferences. The decision to add bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy should be considered separately from hysterectomy rather than assumed as a combined operation. Oophorectomy counseling should reflect gene-specific ovarian cancer risk, menopausal consequences, and competing health priorities: it may be more strongly considered for MLH1 and MSH2 / EPCAM carriers, individualized for MSH6 carriers, and approached with caution for PMS2 carriers because ovarian cancer risk may be near population level. 3 , 4 , 12 Thus, Lynch syndrome counseling should distinguish hysterectomy for endometrial cancer prevention from oophorectomy for ovarian cancer prevention, rather than treating both procedures as a uniform recommendation across all mismatch repair genes.
[w77ee86-17] BRIP1, RAD51C, and RAD51D BRIP1 , RAD51C , and RAD51D are moderate-penetrance tubo-ovarian cancer predisposition genes, and prevention counseling should be age-specific, family-contextual, and explicit about uncertainty. Yang and colleagues estimated cumulative ovarian cancer risks to age 80 years of 11% for RAD51C carriers (95% CI, 6–21%) and 13% for RAD51D carriers (95% CI, 7–23%); risk was higher in carriers with a strong family history of ovarian cancer and was largely concentrated after age 50 years. 26
[w77ee86-18] BRIP1 is also associated with a moderate increase in epithelial ovarian cancer risk, with an estimated cumulative risk to age 80 years of 5.8% (95% CI, 3.6–9.1%), although estimates vary by study design and may be modified by family history and ascertainment context. 27 These estimates support gene-specific counseling but should not be extrapolated to BRCA1 -equivalent surgical timing.
[w77ee86-19] The clinical implication is not that these genes should be managed exactly like BRCA1 . Rather, carriers should be offered counseling about risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy after completion of childbearing, often around age 45-50 years, with adjustment for family history, ancestry, comorbidity, prior breast cancer, and patient values. Earlier surgery may be reasonable in selected families with unusually early ovarian cancer, but genotype alone rarely justifies BRCA1 -like timing. 4 , 12 , 28 , 29
[w77ee86-20] PALB2 PALB2 is a clinically important homologous recombination gene, particularly for breast cancer risk, however, current evidence does not support treating PALB2 as BRCA -equivalent for ovarian cancer.. An international family study estimated PALB2 -associated breast cancer risk to age 80 years at 53% and ovarian cancer risk at 5%. 30 The American College of Medical Genetics and Genomics (ACMG) PALB2 Practice Resource therefore supports personalized management that considers family history and acknowledges a substantially lower ovarian cancer risk than for BRCA1/2 . 31 For tubo-ovarian prevention, PALB2 counseling often centers on whether the individual lifetime tubo-ovarian cancer risk exceeds a prevention threshold and whether surgery is being considered near or after the natural age of menopause. 4 , 28 , 31
[w77ee86-21] Genes with limited ovarian cancer actionability: ATM ATM is a useful example of why gene lists must not be converted into automatic surgical indications. ATM pathogenic variants are established primarily as breast cancer predisposition variants, while ovarian cancer risk estimates remain low, inconsistent, or insufficient to support genotype-alone risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy. 4 , 12 , 32 A patient with an ATM pathogenic variant and a strong family history of ovarian cancer may still warrant individualized risk modeling or specialist review, but the indication is not the ATM result in isolation.
[w77ee86-22] Rare histology-directed syndromes: DICER1 , SMARCA4 , STK11 /Peutz-Jeghers syndrome Rare ovarian histologies may provide stronger clues to an underlying hereditary syndrome than testing strategies designed primarily around common epithelial ovarian cancers. DICER1 tumor predisposition is associated with ovarian Sertoli-Leydig cell tumors and gynandroblastoma, as well as embryonal rhabdomyosarcoma, pleuropulmonary blastoma, thyroid disease, and other manifestations. For these tumors, DICER1 assessment could be performed by immunohistochemistry or by molecular testing, and a pathogenic finding or suggestive phenotype should prompt germline evaluation. 33 , 34 Small cell carcinoma of the ovary, hypercalcemic type, is characterized by SMARCA4 inactivation; loss of SMARCA4 (BRG1) expression by immunohistochemistry can support the diagnosis, and germline SMARCA4 evaluation is indicated for affected patients, with cascade testing for relatives when a germline pathogenic variant is confirmed. 35 , 36 Peutz-Jeghers syndrome, caused by pathogenic variants in STK11 , is associated with sex cord tumor with annular tubules and a broader cancer spectrum involving the gastrointestinal tract, pancreas, breast, cervix, and other gynecologic sites. 37 Together, these entities highlight that hereditary ovarian cancer assessment should extend beyond high-grade serous carcinoma and incorporate histology-directed evaluation when rare tumor types are encountered ( Table 1 ).
[w77ee86-23] Pre-test counseling and test selection Pre-test counseling should answer three practical questions: why testing is recommended, what results may mean, and how results may affect treatment and relatives. Counseling should cover the genes included, the distinction between germline and tumor testing, the possibility of pathogenic, negative, or uncertain results, privacy and insurance considerations applicable to the jurisdiction, and reproductive options. Counseling can be delivered by genetics professionals or by trained oncology teams within a mainstream model, provided escalation pathways exist for complex cases. 38 - 40
[w77ee86-24] Panel selection should be driven by clinical utility. For epithelial ovarian cancer, panels generally include established or clinically relevant ovarian susceptibility genes: BRCA1 , BRCA2 , BRIP1 , RAD51C , RAD51D , PALB2 , MLH1 , MSH2, MSH6 , and PMS2 , with EPCAM deletion analysis where relevant. Genes such as ATM may appear on broader hereditary cancer panels because of associations with other cancers, but current ovarian risk evidence does not support ovarian surgery based on ATM alone. Broader panels may be reasonable when histology, age, personal history, or family history suggests another syndrome, but indiscriminate expansion increases the likelihood of uncertain findings and includes genes with unclear management. Test selection should also consider whether the patient has had prior single-gene testing; many patients tested years ago may not have had large rearrangement analysis or contemporary multigene panel testing. 2 , 10 , 41 , 42
[w77ee86-25] Tumor testing versus germline testing Tumor testing and germline testing answer overlapping but distinct questions. Tumor testing identifies somatic and potential germline alterations that may guide therapy, particularly BRCA1/2 status and homologous recombination deficiency in high-grade non-mucinous disease. 5 , 16 , 43 Germline testing determines inherited risk and is the basis for predictive testing of relatives. A tumor BRCA1/2 pathogenic variant should prompt evaluation for germline origin unless prior germline testing has already clarified this. Conversely, a negative tumor test does not reliably exclude a germline pathogenic variant because tumor assays vary in gene coverage, copy-number detection, sample adequacy and tumor purity, intratumoral heterogeneity or subclonality, allele-fraction thresholds, and bioinformatic filtering and reporting rules. 2 , 5 , 43 Prospective implementation data support parallel approaches. In the SIGNPOST study, 303 of 310 eligible patients underwent parallel germline and somatic testing; germline pathogenic variants were identified in BRCA1/2 in 47 patients (15.5%) and in RAD51C/RAD51D/BRIP1 in 7 patients (2.3%). A somatic-first BRCA strategy would have missed 20% of germline pathogenic variants, including large genomic rearrangements not detected by tumor testing. 44
[w77ee86-26] Tumor-only testing therefore does not replace germline testing when hereditary evaluation is indicated. This distinction should be explicit in multidisciplinary pathways: pathology and oncology workflows can identify therapeutic biomarkers rapidly, but hereditary assessment requires consent, germline assay selection, disclosure planning, documentation, and cascade testing.
[w77ee86-27] Consent models for tumor testing are part of implementation rather than a peripheral administrative issue. Reflex tumor testing may reduce delays, but patients should receive clear information, an opportunity to ask questions, and an opt-out route where local governance supports this approach. In a 2025 UK study involving ovarian cancer patients, clinicians, and charity representatives, 33 of 34 patients (97%) considered reflex tumor testing acceptable, if information and opt-out options were available; this evidence supports pathway redesign but does not remove the need for informed consent for germline testing. 45
[w77ee86-28] Post-test interpretation Pathogenic and likely pathogenic variants Pathogenic and likely pathogenic variants are clinically actionable when the gene-disease association is established, and management recommendations exist. Actionability includes gene-specific cancer risk counseling, risk-reducing surgery where indicated, breast or colorectal surveillance, treatment implications, reproductive options, and cascade testing. The result should be communicated with the gene, variant, inheritance pattern, cancer spectrum, and recommended next steps documented in a way that other clinicians can interpret.
[w77ee86-29] True-negative results A true-negative result occurs when a relative tests negative for a known familial pathogenic variant. This is usually reassuring for the cancers associated with that familial variant, although general population screening and risk factors still apply. The concept should not be confused with a negative multigene panel in an affected proband when no familial variant is known. 46 , 47
[w77ee86-30] Uninformative negative results An uninformative negative result in an affected patient reduces the probability of a currently detectable hereditary syndrome but does not erase familial risk. 46 Residual risk may reflect undiscovered genes or regulatory and noncoding variants not captured by the assay, low-level mosaicism below the assay’s detection or reporting threshold, structural variants missed by older methods, polygenic risk, shared environment, or chance. When suspicion remains high, genetics review may consider updated sequencing, copy-number analysis, tumor-informed testing, or testing of another tissue. Management should return to personal and family history, tumor features, and updated testing availability. 48 , 49
[w77ee86-31] Variants of uncertain significance Variants of uncertain significance are a predictable by-product of multigene testing and must be handled with discipline. The ACMG and the Association of Molecular Pathology (AMP) standards state that a variant of uncertain significance should not be used in clinical decision-making. 50 In hereditary cancer testing, reclassification of variants of uncertain significance occurs over time, but the probability depends on the laboratory, genes tested, variant type, ancestry representation in reference data, and duration of follow-up. In a retrospective cohort from a single commercial hereditary cancer testing laboratory, including 1.45 million individuals and 1.67 million initial tests, Mersch and colleagues found that 7.7% of unique variants of uncertain significance were reclassified; 91.2% of those unique reclassified variants of uncertain significance were downgraded to benign or likely benign, and 8.7% were upgraded to pathogenic or likely pathogenic. 51 Because many variants were observed in more than one individual, 24.9% of all reported variants of uncertain significance observations—not 24.9% of patients—were reclassified. No universally accepted fixed re-review interval applies across laboratories. Reassessment should follow the testing laboratory’s classification and recontact policy and may be triggered by laboratory updates, new family or tumor information, or a management decision that depends on the result. Variants of uncertain significance should not guide risk-reducing surgery or predictive testing of relatives unless reclassified ( Table 2 ).
[w77ee86-32] The clinical response is therefore conservative: do not recommend risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy, mastectomy, intensified screening, or predictive testing of unaffected relatives based on a variant of uncertain significance alone. Relatives with cancer may sometimes be tested in segregation studies coordinated by genetics professionals, but that is different from predictive testing for management. Patients should be told that recontact processes vary and that they should keep contact details current, and should return to genetics if relevant family history changes. 50
[w77ee86-33] Gene-specific risk stratification and timing of prevention Risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy is the most effective established ovarian cancer prevention strategy for carriers whose risk exceeds accepted thresholds, but timing should be individualized. A prevention threshold of approximately 4-5% lifetime ovarian cancer risk has been supported by cost-effectiveness analyses and incorporated into several recommendations. 3 , 12 , 52 That threshold is a starting point for shared decision-making, not an automatic surgical mandate.
[w77ee86-34] For BRCA1 pathogenic variant carriers, risk generally justifies discussion of surgery after childbearing and often from age 35-40 years. For BRCA2 carriers, the later age distribution supports discussion from about age 40-45 years. For RAD51C , RAD51D , BRIP1 , and PALB2 , counseling frequently centers on surgery around age 45-50 years or later, modified by family history and modeled risk. 1 , 3 , 4 , 12 , 26 - 30 For Lynch syndrome, hysterectomy and oophorectomy should be considered as separate prevention decisions: hysterectomy is central for endometrial cancer prevention, while the decision to add bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy is strongest for MLH1 and MSH2 / EPCAM , individualized for MSH6 , and more conservative for PMS2 . 1 , 3 , 12 , 21 , 22 After natural menopause, the balance may favor oophorectomy because premature-menopause harms no longer apply, but the indication should still reflect gene-specific residual risk, prior surgery, comorbidity, and patient preferences.
[w77ee86-35] Risk-reducing early salpingectomy with delayed oophorectomy is attractive because many high-grade serous carcinomas arise in the distal fallopian tube and because delayed oophorectomy may reduce premature menopause. However, it remains investigational because long-term cancer prevention efficacy is not yet established. This strategy should not be conflated with opportunistic salpingectomy during benign pelvic surgery in average-risk patients; opportunistic salpingectomy does not address the residual hereditary ovarian and peritoneal risk created by delaying oophorectomy. Major prospective studies include TUBA-WISP II, which compares salpingectomy with delayed oophorectomy against standard salpingo-oophorectomy in high-risk carriers, and NRG-CC008/SOROCk, which evaluates noninferiority of salpingectomy to salpingo-oophorectomy for risk reduction in BRCA1 carriers. 53 Until prospective data demonstrate that salpingectomy with delayed oophorectomy provides comparable tubo-ovarian cancer prevention, this approach should not be presented as equivalent to risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy and should generally be offered only within a research protocol or structured registry with explicit counseling about residual ovarian and peritoneal cancer risk. 1 , 4 , 12
[w77ee86-36] Role and limitations of individualized risk models Risk models can improve counseling when gene-specific risk is modified by family history, competing risks, and non-genetic factors. BOADICEA, implemented through CanRisk, estimates carrier probabilities and breast, ovarian, and other cancer risks using family history, pathogenic variants, polygenic risk scores, and other factors. 54 , 55 The epithelial tubo-ovarian cancer component incorporates pathogenic variants in BRCA1 , BRCA2 , RAD51C , RAD51D , and BRIP1 , a polygenic risk score, epidemiologic factors, and family history. 56 A 2024 UK Biobank validation reported good calibration for 10-year epithelial tubo-ovarian cancer risk, with improved discrimination when questionnaire-based factors, family history, a 36-variant polygenic risk score, and pathogenic variants were added. 57 Other similar tools answer different clinical questions: BCRAT/Gail and IBIS/Tyrer-Cuzick primarily estimate breast cancer risk, whereas BRCAPRO and Penn II primarily estimate BRCA1/2 carrier probability. They may support breast-risk counseling or testing referral but are not substitutes for ovarian cancer risk models when considering risk-reducing salpingo-oophorectomy. 58
[w77ee86-37] The utility of CanRisk/BOADICEA is greatest when the model clarifies whether a moderate-penetrance carrier or a patient with familial risk is likely to cross a prevention threshold. Model outputs are conditional estimates, not direct measurements of individual risk. They depend on complete family history, correct variant classification, ancestry-specific calibration, population incidence, competing mortality, and available clinical and reproductive factors. Polygenic risk scores add uncertainty because performance is best established in the populations in which they were developed and their effect sizes are more modest than those of high-penetrance pathogenic variants. 54 - 57 , 59 Models should therefore support, not replace, expert clinical judgment and shared decision-making.
[w77ee86-38] Cascade testing and family communication Cascade testing should be treated as a measurable implementation outcome of ovarian cancer genetic testing pathways. A pathogenic variant identified in an affected patient creates a 50% prior probability for each first-degree relative in autosomal dominant syndromes, before considering family structure and intervening test results. Relatives who test positive can receive targeted prevention; relatives who test true negative can often avoid unnecessary high-risk interventions. 11
[w77ee86-39] The traditional model relies heavily on the proband to inform relatives. This protects privacy but produces inconsistent completion of cascade genetic testing among at-risk relatives. In a systematic review and meta-analysis of hereditary breast and ovarian cancer families, Ahsan and colleagues included 30 studies in the meta-analysis and reported pooled cascade genetic testing uptake of 33% among at-risk relatives (95% CI, 25%–42%), with higher uptake among female relatives and first-degree relatives. 60 This estimate refers to completion of cascade genetic testing, not merely family communication or attendance at counseling. Barriers include grief, family estrangement, limited health literacy, uncertainty about what to say, cost, geography, privacy constraints, and failure to treat cascade testing as a measurable outcome of hereditary cancer care. Effective programs use written family letters, genetic counseling support, navigator follow-up, direct contact of relatives where legally and ethically permitted, electronic health record prompts, and low-friction targeted testing. 60 - 63
[w77ee86-40] Figure 1 .
[w77ee86-41] Implementation models Mainstream genetic testing In traditional referral models, patients are usually sent to genetics services for all steps of the testing process — counseling, consent, ordering, and result disclosure — which creates delays and limits reach. Mainstream genetic testing replaces this by embedding pre-test counseling and test ordering within oncology clinics, reserving genetics input for complex cases. This model is particularly appropriate in ovarian cancer because testing has immediate therapeutic and hereditary relevance. Studies of mainstream pathways in ovarian cancer show that trained oncology teams can deliver testing with acceptable patient-reported outcomes and higher reach than genetics-only referral models. 38 - 40 , 44 In a 2025 systematic review and meta-analysis of mainstream genetic testing at or after cancer diagnosis, pooled testing uptake was 91% across cancer types and 93% among ovarian cancer cohorts, with high satisfaction and low decisional conflict, regret, and post-test distress. 39 Economic modeling also supports unselected multigene germline and somatic testing in epithelial ovarian cancer compared with family-history-based approaches, supporting the clinical and economic case for universal testing. 64 Mainstreaming should not mean diluted counseling; it should mean standardized information, clear consent, rapid testing, and reliable genetics backup.
[w77ee86-42] Traceback programs Traceback programs identify patients or families affected by ovarian cancer before universal testing became routine and offer genetic testing retrospectively. These programs are prevention-oriented because many probands are years from diagnosis or deceased; relatives may be the main beneficiaries. Traceback studies demonstrate feasibility but also show that contact, consent, and uptake are challenging, making registry linkage, survivor-sensitive communication, and navigation essential. 61 , 62
[w77ee86-43] Navigation-supported cascade testing Navigation-supported cascade testing treats family follow-up as a care process rather than a passive recommendation. Navigators can help patients understand the result, identify relatives, generate family letters, address cost and insurance barriers, track referrals, and recontact families. Randomized and implementation studies suggest that facilitation can improve communication and testing compared with usual care, although optimal models vary by legal context and health system. 60 , 63
[w77ee86-44] Access issues and underserved populations The impact of the models depends on equitable reach. Implementation failures should be viewed as modifiable system problems. Lack of provider recommendation, referral leakage, long waits, high out-of-pocket cost, language discordance, low digital access, medical mistrust, and limited culturally concordant counseling are not patient deficits; they are design failures. NICE explicitly frames services as responsible for inclusive pathways, training, information, and support for underserved communities. 3 Equity-oriented programs should measure offer rate, completion rate, pathogenic variant detection, time to result, cascade testing uptake, and risk-reducing intervention uptake by ancestry, language, deprivation, geography, and disability status.
[w77ee86-45] Future directions The next phase of hereditary ovarian cancer prevention should integrate diagnosis, treatment, survivorship, and family prevention into a single measurable pathway. First, germline and tumor testing workflows should be coordinated so that treatment biomarkers do not obscure hereditary evaluation. Second, gene-specific risk estimates require continued refinement, especially for moderate-penetrance genes, non-European ancestry groups, and individuals with limited family history. Third, risk-reducing salpingectomy with delayed oophorectomy should continue to be evaluated in adequately powered studies with long-term cancer incidence, quality-of-life, sexual function, menopause, and adherence outcomes to provide high-quality evidence to inform recommendations.
[w77ee86-46] Fourth, risk models need transparent validation across ancestries and clinical settings before polygenic risk scores are used to make high-stakes surgical decisions. Fifth, cascade testing should be recognized as an outcome of ovarian cancer care and supported with infrastructure, not left to chance. Finally, rare histology-directed syndromes require better integration between pathologists, gynecologic oncologists, pediatric and adolescent gynecology, geneticists, and family cancer clinics. Figure 2 .
[w77ee86-47] Conclusions Hereditary ovarian cancer risk assessment has evolved from BRCA1/2-focused counseling into a prevention pathway linking tumor diagnosis to at-risk relatives. Current guideline-based practice is increasingly systematic, gene-specific, and implementation-focused: recognizing BRCA1/2 as central but not exclusive, treating Lynch syndrome by gene, distinguishing moderate- from high-penetrance genes and avoiding over management of variants of uncertain significance or low-evidence genes. Cascade testing should be embedded into ovarian cancer care rather than left as an optional downstream step. For gynecologic oncology clinicians, genetic testing is the entry point; prevention is the goal.

[w3ebad4-1] pmc Arch Gynecol Obstet Arch Gynecol Obstet 365 springeropen 8710213 Archives of Gynecology and Obstetrics 0932-0067 1432-0711 pmc-is-collection-domain yes pmc-collection-title Springer PMC13719812 PMC13719812.1 13719812 13719812 42957917 10.1007/s00404-026-08578-x 8578 1 Research Surgical quality and long-term outcomes in advanced ovarian cancer: a population based registry study from Hamburg, Germany 2014–2024 https://orcid.org/0009-0005-4378-5885 Annemarie Schultz annemarie.schultz@bwfg.hamburg.de 1 Frederik Peters 1 Anna Jaeger 2 Barbara Schmalfeldt b.schmalfeldt@uke.de 2 1 Hamburg Cancer Registry, Hamburg, Germany 2 https://ror.org/01zgy1s35 grid.13648.38 0000 0001 2180 3484 Department of Gynaecology, University Medical Centre Hamburg-Eppendorf, Hamburg, Germany 29 10 2026 2026 313 1 524388 302 13 1 2026 15 9 2026 29 10 2026 31 10 2026 04 11 2026 © The Author(s) 2026 2026 https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/ Open Access This article is licensed under a Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International License, which permits use, sharing, adaptation, distribution and reproduction in any medium or format, as long as you give appropriate credit to the original author(s) and the source, provide a link to the Creative Commons licence, and indicate if changes were made. Purpose To evaluate the association of surgical quality and survival in patients with advanced ovarian cancer in a real-world setting.
[w3ebad4-2] Methods This population-based registry study analysed FIGO stage III/IV ovarian cancer patients in Hamburg, Germany (2014–2024). Patients were categorized by surgical outcome (no macroscopic residuals vs. macroscopic residuals). Kaplan–Meier functions and Cox proportional hazards models estimated associations with survival outcomes.
[w3ebad4-3] Results The cohort comprised 576 patients (median age 65 years, median follow-up 57 months). No macroscopic residuals were achieved in 370 patients (64.2%). In multivariable analysis, no macroscopic residuals were associated with reduced mortality risk (HR 0.62, 95% CI: 0.42–0.90) and reduced progression risk (HR 0.68, 95% CI: 0.49–0.94). Advanced FIGO stage, poor performance status, and increasing age were independent predictors of worse outcomes.
[w3ebad4-4] Conclusion Achieving no macroscopic residuals remains a critical prognostic factor in advanced ovarian cancer. The rate of 64.2% demonstrates that optimal surgical outcomes are achievable in routine practice, though opportunities for improvement remain compared to specialized centres. Supplementary Information The online version contains supplementary material available at https://doi.org/10.1007/s00404-026-08578-x .
[w3ebad4-5] Keywords Cytoreductive surgery Survival analysis Real-world evidence Cancer registry Complete resection FIGO stage III/IV Universitätsklinikum Hamburg-Eppendorf (UKE) (5411) Open Access funding enabled and organized by Projekt DEAL. pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access yes pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript no pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement yes pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes pmc-license-ref CC BY issue-copyright-statement © Springer-Verlag GmbH Germany, part of Springer Nature 2026 What does this study add to the clinical work?
[w3ebad4-6] This population-based study confirms that achieving no macroscopic residuals remains the most strongly associated modifiable prognostic factor in advanced ovarian cancer, with significantly reduced mortality and progression risk. The complete resection rate of 64.2% in routine practice demonstrates achievable outcomes while identifying opportunities for quality improvement compared to specialized centres.
[w3ebad4-7] Introduction Ovarian cancer remains the gynaecological malignancy with the highest mortality rate, despite continuous improvements in surgical and systemic treatment options over the years [ 1 ]. Although early-stage disease is highly curable, almost two thirds of the patients suffer from advanced stage of disease (stage III/IV) at initial diagnosis [ 1 ], with over 75% of patients with late-stage ovarian cancer dying of their disease [ 2 – 4 ]. This pattern is confirmed by current German registry data, showing that 77% of ovarian cancers are diagnosed in advanced FIGO stages III/IV, with a stark survival difference between early (86.1%) and advanced stages (37.2%) [ 5 ].
[w3ebad4-8] The quality of therapy is a key prognostic parameter in ovarian cancer, particularly residual tumour after debulking surgery [ 6 ]. Complete macroscopic resection has become the standard of care, with rates of 70–80% now achievable in specialized centres [ 7 , 8 ]. Platinum-taxane-based chemotherapy is the second pillar in advanced ovarian cancer treatment [ 9 ].
[w3ebad4-9] Maintenance therapy with bevacizumab and/or PARP inhibitors prolongs progression-free survival [ 10 – 16 ], yet the combined impact of surgical quality and systemic therapy on outcomes remains incompletely understood. Most clinical trials evaluate these interventions separately, providing limited real-world evidence on their synergistic effects. Understanding how complete resection and optimal systemic therapy interact is crucial for treatment optimization and patient counselling, particularly as treatment patterns vary substantially across different healthcare settings and patient populations.
[w3ebad4-10] Recent German quality assurance data demonstrate improving outcomes in advanced ovarian cancer, with median progression-free survival increasing from 12.7 to 20.5 months between 2004 and 2016, attributed to improved surgical techniques and systemic therapy advances [ 17 ]. Comorbidities and frailty are substantially associated with reduced treatment adherence, with about one third of patients not receiving optimal therapy [ 17 – 19 ]. Histological subtype also influences prognosis, with high-grade serous cancers showing worse outcomes [ 20 ].
[w3ebad4-11] The German Cancer Society’s audit report indicates complete resection rates of 73.3% in certified centres [ 21 , 22 ], comparable to specialized centres, such as the Mayo Clinic (75%) [ 21 ].
[w3ebad4-12] While the prognostic importance of complete cytoreduction is well-established, contemporary population-based evidence from routine clinical practice remains limited, particularly in the era of bevacizumab and PARP inhibitor availability, and for unselected patient populations including elderly and comorbid patients typically underrepresented in clinical trials. Hamburg’s metropolitan healthcare context, with universal insurance coverage and short distances to multiple certified centres, provides a setting in which achievable real-world surgical quality can be meaningfully assessed.
[w3ebad4-13] In this population-based study, we aimed to quantify the real-world survival outcomes associated with surgical quality in patients with advanced ovarian cancer. Our primary objective was to compare overall survival and progression-free survival between patients with no macroscopic residuals versus those with macroscopic residuals, while accounting for systemic therapy and other prognostic factors. Through this approach, we sought to evaluate the prognostic importance of complete cytoreduction in routine clinical practice and identify potential strategies for improving surgical outcomes.
[w3ebad4-14] Methods Data source The Hamburg Cancer Registry captures all cancer cases with mandatory reporting since 2013 and estimated completeness > 95% [ 23 , 24 ].
[w3ebad4-15] Patients We included patients with advanced ovarian cancer diagnosis (Fédération Internationale de Gynécologie et d’Obstétrique (FIGO) stage III or IV [ 25 ]) between 2014 and 2024 registered in Hamburg, Germany, according to the International Classification of Diseases 10 German Modification (ICD10-GM) with code C56, C57.0, C57.4, C57.8, C48.1, C48.2, C48.8. We excluded cases with primary ovarian tumours before 2014 (based on data from the epidemiological registry spanning over the time 1990–2024), patients without residence in Hamburg, age at diagnosis < 18 or > 99, without surgery, or unknown systemic therapy. Only surgeries with curative intention were included. In cases of multiple tumour reports per patient, only the first case was considered. Patients with unknown residual disease status after surgery were not excluded, but were retained in the analytic cohort, with missing values addressed through multiple imputation.
[w3ebad4-16] Study variables The primary variable was surgical quality, defined as the presence or absence of macroscopic residual tumour after surgery, as documented in the German oncological base dataset (oBDS) [ 26 ]. Patients were grouped into no macroscopic residuals, defined as no grossly visible tumour remaining after surgery regardless of microscopic margin status, versus macroscopic residual disease, defined as any grossly visible tumour remaining after surgery. For FIGO stage IV disease, overall residual disease status was used, while for FIGO stage III, local residual disease status was prioritized. Surgical treatments were included if initiated within six months before or after diagnosis. The 6-month window was chosen based on observed treatment patterns in the registry, in which most patients initiated primary therapy within this timeframe. The window extends prior to diagnosis to account for cases in which surgery preceded the formal registry diagnosis date, as histological confirmation and reporting may follow the surgical procedure. In patients with multiple surgeries within the window, the date of the last surgery was used for further analysis, and the best achieved residual disease status across all surgeries was recorded. Primary cytoreductive surgery was defined as surgery performed prior to the initiation of first-line chemotherapy; interval cytoreductive surgery as surgery performed following neoadjuvant chemotherapy, with an interval of at least 29 days between diagnosis and first surgery. Systemic treatments were included if initiated within 6 months before or up to 12 months after diagnosis to capture first-line interventions. Only surgeries with curative treatment intention were included, as documented in the treatment intention field of the German oncological base dataset (oBDS), which is a mandatory reporting item for all surgical interventions. Systemic therapy was categorized as bevacizumab-containing versus other regimens. We focused on bevacizumab as it was consistently documented, unlike PARP inhibitors which were newly introduced during the study period [ 11 , 13 ]. Variables included in this analysis were calendar year (grouped in 2014–2016, 2017–2019 and 2020–2024), FIGO stage (III or IV) age at diagnosis, histological subgroup according to the definitions of the World Health Organization [ 27 ] (endometroid carcinoma (ICD-O-3: 8380, 8474), low grade serous adenocarcinoma (ICD-O-3: 8460), high grade serous adenocarcinoma (ICD-O-3: 8461, 8441) and others (e.g. clear cell carcinoma (ICD-O-3: 8310) and mucinous (ICD-O-3: 8480)), performance status based on ECOG performance status (0 = fully active; 1 = restricted in physically strenuous activity, but ambulatory; > 1 = limited self-care capacity or bedridden) [ 28 ], grouped as 0–1 vs > 1. Where Karnofsky Performance Status was documented instead, values were converted to ECOG scores according to standard oBDS crosswalk criteria. Treatment in a certified gynaecological oncology centre was included as a binary variable.
[w3ebad4-17] Overall survival was defined as time from the date of the last surgery within six months after diagnosis to death from any cause within 8 years of follow-up, as cancer-specific mortality data were not consistently available in the registry. Progression-free survival was defined as time from the date of the last surgery within six months after diagnosis to disease progression or death from any cause, whichever occurred first, within 8 years of follow-up. Patients without events were censored at 8 years of follow-up or at the end of the study period (December 31, 2024).
[w3ebad4-18] Statistical analysis Descriptive statistics were presented using proportions for discrete variables and median for continuous variables. For estimating the impact of prognostic factors on PFS and OS, Kaplan–Meier functions with log rank tests and Cox proportional hazard regression models were applied. The proportional hazards assumption was assessed for all covariables using the global test and variable-specific scaled Schoenfeld residuals (cox.zph). Multiple imputation by chained equations with 20 iterations for 5 copies of the data were used to account for missing values in ECOG performance status, nodal status, and residual disease classification. The imputation model included FIGO stage, age at diagnosis, diagnosis period, histological subtype, ECOG performance status, treatment centre, surgical complications, interval between diagnosis and surgery, surgery type (primary vs. interval), nodal status, and residual disease status. Results pooled based on Rubin’s rules were presented as hazard ratios with 95% confidence intervals. We did not adjust for multiple testing.
[w3ebad4-19] Eight sensitivity analyses were performed to test the robustness of our findings: (1) complete case analysis excluding patients with missing data, (2) analysis restricted to patients treated in certified centres, (3) analysis excluding patients who did not achieve a follow-up of 12 months after diagnosis, (4) propensity score matched analysis using 1:1 nearest-neighbour matching without replacement, balancing baseline characteristics (FIGO stage, diagnosis period, age, histological subtype, ECOG performance status, and treatment centre) between surgical outcome groups, (5) three-year follow-up analysis, (6) analysis excluding patients aged ≥ 75 years, and (7) analysis excluding patients who died within 365 days after diagnosis, and (8) analysis restricted to patients with no documented neoadjuvant chemotherapy prior to surgery.
[w3ebad4-20] R version 4.3.1 was used to perform all analyses and plots (R Core Team, 2023, https://www.R-project.org/ ). We adhered to the Strengthening the Reporting of Observational Studies in Epidemiology (STROBE) for reporting our results [ 29 ].
[w3ebad4-21] Results Patient characteristics A total of 576 patients were included (median age 65 years; median follow-up 57 months for OS and 55 months for PFS; Fig. 1 , Table 1 ). High-grade serous histology (74.1) and FIGO stage III (65.8%) predominated. Within eight years, 295 patients (51.2) died and 353 (61.3%) experienced progression or death. No macroscopic residuals were achieved in 370 patients (64.2%), while 128 patients (22.2%) had macroscopic residual disease and 78 patients (13.5%) had unknown resection status. Among patients with no macroscopic residuals, 180 (48.6%) received bevacizumab-containing first-line therapy and 190 (51.4%) received platinum-taxane or other regimens without bevacizumab. In the macroscopic residual disease group, 66 (51.6%) received bevacizumab maintenance and 62 (48.4%) received other regimens.

[w3ebad4-25] Baseline differences between groups included higher FIGO stage IV rates in the macroscopic residuals group (46.1% vs 30.8%) and older median age (69 vs 64 years). The rate of no macroscopic residuals (64.2%) reflects real-world treatment patterns.
[w3ebad4-26] Unadjusted survival outcomes by treatment group Marked differences were observed in both overall survival and progression-free survival between surgical outcome groups (Fig. 2 A). Patients with no macroscopic residuals demonstrated superior outcomes compared to those with macroscopic residual disease across both endpoints. Median overall survival was 56 months in the no macroscopic residuals group compared to 33 months in the macroscopic residual disease group. Median progression-free survival was 36 months in the no macroscopic residuals group versus 20 months in the macroscopic residual disease group (Fig. 2 A). Fig. 2 A Overall survival and progression-free survival of patients with advanced ovarian cancer by surgical outcome ( n = 576) B Overall survival and progression-free survival of patients with advanced ovarian cancer by surgical outcome, after propensity score matching based on FIGO stage, diagnosis period, age, histological subtype, ECOG performance status, and treatment centre ( n = 260)
[w3ebad4-27] Propensity score matching ( n = 260, 130 pairs), no macroscopic residuals remained associated with improved overall survival (HR 0.72, 95% CI: 0.52–1.00) and progression-free survival (HR 0.84, 95% CI: 0.62–1.13), consistent in direction with the primary analysis, though not reaching statistical significance, likely reflecting the reduced statistical power of the matched subsample (Fig. 2 B).
[w3ebad4-28] Multivariable analysis of factors associated with overall survival In multivariable analysis for overall survival (Fig. 3 A), no macroscopic residuals were associated with a 38% reduced mortality risk compared to macroscopic residual disease (HR 0.62, 95% CI: 0.42–0.90). Among clinical factors, FIGO stage III was associated with a 38% reduced mortality risk compared to stage IV ( HR 0.62, 95% CI: 0.45–0.86), and good performance status (ECOG 0–1) reduced mortality risk by 37% (HR 0.64, 95% CI: 0.43–0.94). Primary surgery was associated with a 30% reduced mortality risk compared to interval surgery (HR 0.70, 95% CI: 0.47–1.02), though it did not reach statistical significance. Fig. 3 Multivariable analysis of factors associated with overall survival A and progression-free survival B in patients with advanced ovarian cancer
[w3ebad4-29] Multivariable analysis of factors associated with progression-free survival In multivariable analysis for progression-free survival (Fig. 3 B), no macroscopic residuals were associated with a 32% reduced risk of progression or death compared to macroscopic residual disease (HR 0.68, 95% CI: 0.49–0.94). FIGO stage III was associated with a 31% reduced risk compared to stage IV (HR 0.69, 95% CI: 0.54–0.87), and good performance status (ECOG 0–1) was associated with a 32% reduced risk (HR 0.68, 95% CI: 0.48–0.95). Primary surgery showed the strongest association with a 45% reduced risk compared to interval surgery (HR 0.55, 95% CI: 0.39–0.78).
[w3ebad4-30] The proportional hazards assumption was satisfied for the primary exposure, residual disease status, in both models (scaled Schoenfeld residuals: OS p = 0.785, PFS p = 0.161), with no systematic trend over time. The global test was significant for the overall survival model ( p = 0.021). This reflected time-varying effects of age ( p = 0.001), histology ( p = 0.002), surgical complications ( p = 0.005), and nodal status ( p = 0.049), which are well-recognised in long-term observational cohorts and do not affect the estimate for the primary exposure. An accelerated failure time model with a Weibull distribution yielded concordant estimates for the primary exposure (OS HR 0.66, 95% CI 0.46–0.92; PFS HR 0.68, 95% CI 0.49–0.93), confirming robustness to this assumption.
[w3ebad4-31] Sensitivity analyses Eight sensitivity analyses confirmed robust associations (Supplementary Table S1 ). Complete case analysis ( n = 336), certified centres only ( n = 463), propensity score matching ( n = 260), three-year follow-up, and excluding patients aged ≥ 75 years, and an analysis restricted to patients classified as undergoing primary cytoreductive surgery (no documented neoadjuvant chemotherapy prior to surgery; n ≈ 506) all showed consistent HRs between 0.51 and 0.72 for overall survival and 0.57–0.84 for progression-free survival.
[w3ebad4-32] Discussion This population-based study demonstrates that no macroscopic residuals (achieved in 64.2% of patients) were associated with 38% reduced mortality (HR 0.62) and 32% reduced progression risk (HR 0.68), consistent across all sensitivity analyses.
[w3ebad4-33] When comparing our survival outcomes to national reference data, our results suggest reasonable treatment quality despite the lower complete resection rate. While direct comparison with Robert Koch Institute cancer statistics 2019/2020 has limitations given our broader inclusion criteria (C56, C57.x, C48.x versus RKI’s C56 only), the reported relative 5-year survival rates for ovarian cancer of 42% for FIGO stage III and 21% for FIGO stage IV [ 30 ] provide useful context. Our survival patterns by resection status appear broadly consistent with these national benchmarks, suggesting that overall treatment quality in Hamburg achieves outcomes within expected ranges despite the documented challenges in achieving complete resection.
[w3ebad4-34] Our results strongly align with established evidence demonstrating the critical importance of optimal cytoreduction in advanced ovarian cancer [ 6 ]. The significant survival benefits observed across both overall survival and progression-free survival reinforce that surgical quality remains the most strongly associated modifiable prognostic factor in this disease [ 2 – 4 ]. The consistency of our findings across multiple sensitivity analyses, including propensity score matching with 260 patients, complete case analysis with 336 patients, and subgroup analyses, supports the robustness of the association between no macroscopic residuals and improved outcomes. As an observational analysis restricted to measured variables, however, residual confounding cannot be excluded, in particular from factors not captured in the registry, such as comorbidity burden, tumour burden (e.g., peritoneal cancer index), surgical complexity, and surgeon or institutional experience.
[w3ebad4-35] Our rate of no macroscopic residuals (64.2%) differs from quality assurance data reporting rates of 73.3–85% [ 17 , 23 ]. However, these rates typically include residual disease < 1 cm, which differs from the strict criterion of no grossly visible residual tumour. The differences may reflect selection bias in certification data, broader patient inclusion in our registry, and incomplete documentation. Standardized documentation of surgical outcomes is essential for accurate quality assessment [ 21 , 31 ].
[w3ebad4-36] While the prognostic importance of optimal cytoreduction is well-established, our study contributes important population-based evidence on treatment patterns and outcomes in routine clinical practice. Unlike highly selected clinical trial populations, our cohort reflects the full spectrum of patients treated in a metropolitan cancer care system, including elderly patients and those with comorbidities who are typically underrepresented in interventional studies. The rate of no macroscopic residuals (64.2%) documented here provides crucial insights into real-world treatment gaps and identifies opportunities for quality improvement initiatives. Whether this reflects patient selection (e.g., more patients deemed unsuitable for aggressive cytoreduction), surgical practice patterns, or documentation issues warrants further investigation.
[w3ebad4-37] The high rate of missing ECOG (33.0%) highlights challenges in routine documentation, emphasizing the need for improved surgical outcome reporting as recommended by clinical guidelines [ 6 ]. Regarding the median age at initial diagnosis (65 years), FIGO stage distribution (65.8% FIGO III, 34.2% FIGO IV) and histological subtype (74.1% high-grade serous) our results are comparable to previous studies [ 31 , 32 ].
[w3ebad4-38] Interestingly, the percentage of patients treated in experienced, certified gynaecological oncology centres in our study was high with 80.4% compared to other studies. This could be attributed to the fact that the study region Hamburg represents a metropolitan area with short distances to specialised care and a generally high coverage rate of health services. Notably, approximately 25% of patients did not receive documented systemic chemotherapy, a proportion higher than expected based on guideline recommendations. This may reflect incomplete documentation, contraindications in frail patients, or patient refusal, and warrants further investigation.
[w3ebad4-39] Sensitivity analysis excluding patients ≥ 75 years showed particularly strong associations (HR 0.51 for OS, HR 0.57 for PFS), suggesting surgical quality may be especially strongly associated with outcomes in younger patients [ 4 , 19 , 33 ]. The propensity score matched analysis yielded results consistent in direction with the primary analysis; the absence of statistical significance likely reflects the reduced power of the matched subsample of 260 patients. These findings underscore the importance of individualizing treatment decisions based on patient condition and functional status.
[w3ebad4-40] Limitations and strengths Several important limitations warrant acknowledgment. First, we lacked information on BRCA1/2 mutations and homologous recombination deficiency status. Second, PARP inhibitor documentation was incomplete. Third, the number of patients with macroscopic residual disease ( n = 128) limits statistical power.
[w3ebad4-41] Fourth, as an observational study, unmeasured confounders including surgeon experience and comorbidity profiles may influence outcomes. Although all patients had undergone surgery with curative intent, detailed comorbidity data were not available in the registry. Prior malignancies, ECOG performance status, and surgical complications served as partial proxies for patient frailty; however, surgeon experience and institutional case volume were not captured and may have independently influenced outcomes. Furthermore, as clinical cancer registration in Hamburg was established in 2013, documentation completeness for surgical procedures and clinical variables may have been lower in the earlier years of the study period, potentially introducing information bias. Additionally, competing risks analysis for non-cancer mortality was not performed, which may be relevant given the proportion of elderly patients. Multiple sensitivity analyses and two survival endpoints were evaluated without adjustment for multiple testing. Results should, therefore, be interpreted in the context of the overall consistency of findings across analyses. Finally, the high proportion of patients treated in certified gynaecological oncology centres (80.4%) reflects Hamburg’s specific metropolitan infrastructure and may not be representative of broader national or international practice patterns. This contextual characteristic should be considered when generalising these findings to other settings.
[w3ebad4-42] Notable strengths include the population-based design capturing real-world practice, high registry completeness (> 95%) [ 24 ], assessment of both PFS and OS, multiple sensitivity analyses including propensity score matching ( n = 260), and use of multiple imputation to mitigate bias from missing data.
[w3ebad4-43] Conclusion This population-based study demonstrates that achieving no macroscopic residuals remains strongly associated with improved survival outcomes in advanced ovarian cancer. No macroscopic residuals were associated with significantly reduced mortality risk and reduced risk of progression or death, with consistent findings confirmed across multiple sensitivity analyses. The rate of achieving no macroscopic residuals in this population-based cohort demonstrates that optimal surgical outcomes are attainable in routine practice, though opportunities for further improvement remain when compared to specialized centres.
[w3ebad4-44] The consistency of surgical quality effects across all analytical approaches reinforces that avoiding macroscopic residual disease remains the most strongly associated modifiable prognostic factor in advanced ovarian cancer. These findings emphasize the continued importance of surgical quality as strongly associated with outcomes and support efforts to centralize care in high-volume centres with expertise in complex cytoreductive surgery. Additionally, improved documentation of surgical outcomes in routine practice is essential for accurate quality assessment and monitoring of treatment improvements over time.
